Rec.</em> copy). The “Rozel-gate” of Herbert (1665), “Rouselgout” of Fryer (1698), “Cape Russelgate” of Anglo-Indians, Ras-el-Hadd, <em>i.e.</em> “Cape of the (S.E.) Limit,” the low and sandy promontory of Arabia Felix which gives entrance to the Gulf of Oman.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body">Or any porte of the cost (<em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">8.</a> <span class="fn-body">Or (<em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Vnreasonable (<em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy); this is evidently the right rendering.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cp. <em>Hamlet</em>, Act I., Scene 2:—<br class="fn-para">“In grace whereof,<br>No jocund health that Denmark drinks to-day,<br>But the great cannon to the clouds shall tell,<br>And the king’s rouse the heavens shall bruit again,<br>Re-speaking earthly thunder.”</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The words “to be kept . . . . Middleton” have been omitted in the <em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Preiudice (<em>Mar. Rec. copy</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Containe” in <em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy,—an evident blunder.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 311.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not in <em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">And (<em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy concludes:— Signed by S<sup>r</sup> Thomas Smith Gouernor Robert Bell Robert Middellton Thomas Style Hughe Hamersly Robert Offley.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Rare thinges as live birdes &amp;c (<em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy continues: “that retourn to see them carefully keept and “delivered vs, And w<sup>th</sup>all yf you shall knowe any of the marryners or other to haue “any such, w<sup>ch</sup> you cannot procure from them for reason then that you send vs “notice thereof to thend wee may take order therin accordingly.” The whole of this postscript is, in the <em>Mar. Rec.</em> copy, incorporated in the last paragraph of the commission.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">These are the royal letters carried out in the <em>Sixth Voyage</em> for presentation to various Eastern princes and governors.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 219.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bales?</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>i.e.</em> Neira, one of the Banda Islands.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The loss of the 1610-14 Court Book prevents the giving of details as to the circumstances under which this warrant was applied for. It is interesting as an example of the strictness of the currency laws of the period.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The original and duplicate of this commission (with the remains of the Great Seal still attached) are preserved among the <em>Parchment Records</em> (Nos. 6 and 7) in the India Office. There are also Minutes of the Grant at the Public Record Office (see <em>Cal. of State Papers</em>, <em>E. Indies</em>, 1513-1616, p. 205).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Captain Hippon’s Commission for the <em>Seventh Voyage</em>, 1611 (see also <em>Cal. of State Papers</em>, <em>E. Indies</em>, 1513-1616, page 208).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 114, note 3.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “as.”</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This should be “we.”</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">These two words appear to have been transposed in copying.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 52, note 1.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Peter Floris (here miswritten Ploris) and Lucas Antheumis (or Antheunis) were two Dutch merchants to whom was entrusted the management of the Company’s <em>Seventh Voyage</em> (1611), which was especially intended to open up trade on the Coromandel Coast and at Patani and Siam. Probably both of them—and certainly Floris—had had experience of trade in the Indies; and, as will be seen from the present document, they had a considerable stake in the enterprise, contributing one-eighth of the total capital. The voyage proved to be a success, but was fatal to Floris, who died within two months of his return to London (1615). Antheumis, who had been left at Siam, remained there until September, 1615, when he proceeded to Patani, and thence to Masulipatam, finally reaching London in September, 1618.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In the absence of any definite information as to its nature and meaning, the symbol which in the original occurs here has been printed as a “y”, the modern (written) form of that letter giving perhaps the best idea of its shape. What it really is, and what its significance, are questions to which, in spite of much kind help from Mr. Jeayes (of the British Museum), Mr. Noel Sainsbury (of the Public Record Office), Mr. F. G. Hilton Price (of Child’s Bank), and others, no answer can yet be given. Some experts consider it an “n” with the tail turned up (meaning “nett” or some similar word); others hold it to be a contraction for <em>videlicet</em>; others again regard it as a “z”, or some conventional sign for money. Bearing in mind the nationality of the writers, it may be conjectured that it is an old Dutch mercantile symbol, perverted possibly beyond recognition by the ignorance of the copyist.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This commission, though apparently completed and communicated to those concerned, seems to have been superseded by a fresh draft, which will be found on page 379. Possibly Floris and Antheumis were dissatisfied with it in its present form.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Of this band of factors, Essington, on the death of Hippon in July, 1612, became captain of the <em>Globe</em>, and held that post until his death at Masulipatam in May, 1614; Denton became in turn chief at Patani and at Masulipatam, returning home in 1621; and Chauncey, after being stationed at various factories, died at Patani in the autumn of 1615.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Laws.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Fights are the wast-cloaths, which hang round about the ship, to hinder men “from being seen in fight; or any place wherein men may cover themselves, &amp; yet “use their arms.” (Phillips’s <em>World of Words</em>, quoted in Nares’ <em>Glossary</em>.)<br class="fn-para">“Being neere unto it, they spread their fights, which were of thicke mattes, and “began to defend themselues.”—(Hakluyt’s account of the Dutch voyage of 1595.)<br class="fn-para">“Clap on more sails; pursue; up with your fights; Give fire! She is my prize, or ocean whelm them all!”<br class="fn-para"><em>Merry Wives of Windsor</em>, Act II., Sc. 2.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Home.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “sigilli.”</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This appears to be a revised commission, substituted for the preceding document, and is probably the authoritative version of the Company’s instructions for the voyage. Here again one finds cause to mourn the loss of the Court Minutes for this period.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Book-keeper.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 421.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “of.”</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">These appear to be copies of Royal letters carried out in the <em>Eighth Voyage</em> (see page 396, note).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Enterteyne” seems to be meant.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 219, note.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bales?</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 434.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the <em>Eighth Voyage</em> under Saris, especially memorable for the opening up of trade with Japan, and the establishment of a factory in that country.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 92, note.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 33, note.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a minute of this royal commission in the Public Record Office, under date 30th March, 1611 (see <em>Cal. of State Papers, E. Indies, 1513-1616</em>, p. 215). No copy of it is known; but it doubtless followed on the lines of previous commissions, of which there are several examples in the present volume.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Tempest Peacock was left at the newly-established factory at Firando, in Japan, as next in command to Cocks and Adams. In March, 1614, he set out on a trading expedition to Cochin China, and was there murdered by the natives not long after his arrival.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Richard Cocks held charge of the English factory at Firando from its establishment in 1613 till its withdrawal ten years later, and died on his way home, 24th March, 1624. His <em>Diary</em> whilst in Japan has been published by the Hakluyt Society, under the editorship of Mr. E. Maunde Thompson, of the British Museum.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Richard Wickham went out first to the Indies as a factor in the <em>Union</em> in the <em>Fourth Voyage</em>. At Zanzibar he was captured by the natives and handed over to the Portuguese. By them he was carried to Goa, where he met the traveller François Pyrard. In 1610 Wickham was sent, with other European captives, to Portugal, whence he made his way back to England in time to offer his services for this <em>Eighth Voyage</em>. Saris left him with Cocks at the factory at Firando; and his letter-book during a portion of his service there (1614-16) is still preserved among the India Office Records. Wickham left Japan in 1618 for Bantam, whence he proceeded to Jacatra, where he died not long after, worth, it was said, a large sum of money, accumulated in the course of his trading.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Edward Camden was left by Capt. Saris at Bantam as chief factor for the goods of the <em>Eighth Voyage</em>, and died there, probably some time in 1613.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">George Ball was left by Saris at Bantam, where in March, 1617, he succeeded Barkeley as President. Loud complaints were soon made as to his excessive private trading, and in 1621 he was brought home a prisoner, and an action entered against him in the Star Chamber for 70,000<em>l</em>. The proceedings, however, dragged on very slowly, and the case had not been finally settled when he died (about the beginning of 1625).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The reference is to the murder of Rowles and his companions in Madagascar (see page 228, note).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 118, note.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-131" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-131" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">All along the well-wooded narrow Konkan Coast, and through all the wide extended fertile plains of Southern Gujerat, the Hindu women of Western India, of the higher castes, have, or had in my time, 22 to 37 years ago, a marked partiality for robes (<em>saris</em>) of a single coloured text, contrasted with a pretext (παρυφή, <em>clavus</em>) of the complementary, or, indeed, any other harmonising colour; the Hindu weavers being able to bring any two colours, even pea-green and purple, and scarlet and purple, into perfect unison. In the Southern Konkan particularly, I found that, besides robes of Cardinal white bordered with gold (χρυσοπάρυφος, <em>chrysoclavus</em>), the most commonly worn were of marigold or saffron yellow (κροκωτός) edged with Mazarine blue and buff, or primrose yellow edged with pale blue, both universally favourite combinations, and of pea green with purple (παραλουργής), and blazing <span class="folio-turn">[p. 406]</span> scarlet (<em>κοκκοβαφής</em>) with pistachio green, and lastly of carnation, with a pretext of either apple green, interthreaded with gold, or deep blue separated from the text of carnation by a strip of gold, a chromatic concordance of exquisite refinement. And beyond doubt it was the great charm of these Indian stuffs that first made carnation a fashionable colour in England. It takes its English name from “the Carnation” [in German <em>das Incarnat</em>], or Double Clove Pink, otherwise known as “the Whitsuntide [in German <em>Pfingsten</em>] Gilliflower,” the <em>Dianthus</em> [<em>Διὸς ἄνθος</em>, flos Jovis] <em>Caryophyllus</em> of botanists; and this name was not given to it on account of its colour, but is corrupted from its earlier English name of “Coronation,” given to it on account of the use of its flowers for festive chaplets. Thus Spenser in <em>The Shepheardes Calender</em>, April, 134-145:—<br class="fn-para">“Bring hither the Pincke [<em>Dianthus hortensis</em>] and purple Cullambine With Gelliflowres [single ‘Clove Pink’]; Bring <span class="sc">coronations</span> and Sops in wine [scented ‘Clove Pink’] Worne of Paramoures; Strowe me the ground with Daffadowndillies, And Cowslips, and Kingcups, and loved Lillies: The pretie Pawnce [‘Pansy’], And the Chevisaunce [<em>Cheiranthus Cheiri</em>, the ‘Wallflower’], Shall match with the fayre floure Delice [Fleur de Lys (<em>i.e.</em> Louis VII.), the ‘Iris’].”<br class="fn-para">But already the proper English name of the double <em>Dianthus Caryophyllus</em> was being contracted to “Carnation.” Lyte, in his <em>Niewe Herbal</em>, 1578, of the tautologically named “Clove Gillofers,” writes, B. ii., ch. 7:—“The greatest and bravest sort of them are called <em>Coronations</em> or <em>Cornations</em>”; and, again, Shakespeare in <em>Love’s Labour Lost</em> (1592), III., 1:—“Pray you sir, how much <em>Carnation</em> ribbon may a man buy for a remuneration”: that is, as I take it, “ribbon (<em>red</em> strips or fillets of silk) for chaplets,” or other “head gear”; although even at this early stage of the decay of the name it may have acquired the meaning of red, from its erroneous association with, and, indeed, express derivation from, the Italian <em>incarnadino</em> and <em>incarnatino</em> (Florio, 1598), literally, “raw-meat-coloured,” or “incarnadined”; a word which, until it obtained a poetical significance through its erroneous association with “the Coronation,”</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This should evidently be “reserve from,” as on page 338. The whole passage seems to have been copied with a few alterations from the corresponding paragraph in the Commission for the <em>Sixth Voyage</em>; hence, probably, the further blunder at the end of the paragraph, where it is implied that only one ship would be sent home from Surat, whereas just before it is stated [that, if possible, all three, or two out of the three, were to be laden there, and despatched for England.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-133" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-133" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 286.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-134" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-134" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Some words have been omitted here; the gap may be supplied from the corresponding passage in the commission for the <em>Sixth Voyage</em> (see page 340), which is followed almost word for word.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-135" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-135" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">To?</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-136" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-136" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Or?</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-137" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-137" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The story of William Adams, who lived in Japan for twenty years, and was “in “such favor with two emperors of Japan as never was any Christian,” has often been told, and may be found admirably summarized in the <em>Dictionary of National Biography</em>. He sailed in 1598 as pilot to a Dutch fleet bound for the Indies, but his vessel got separated from her consorts, and with difficulty reached the coast of Japan in April, 1600. Adams was sent up to the court, where he speedily succeeded in gaining much influence over the emperor. In October 1611, he placed himself in communication with the Company’s factors at Bantam; but his story was already known through the Dutch, and, as we see, the Company at home had some months <span class="folio-turn">[p. 413]</span> before made arrangements to open up trade with Japan. On Saris’s arrival, in June, 1613, Adams accompanied him to court, and assisted in procuring the necessary privileges. He did not, however, avail himself of the opportunity of returning home in the <em>Clove</em>, but took service with the Company as second in command of the factory established by Saris at Firando. He made several voyages to Siam and other places in the Company’s interests, and even after he had quitted their service in 1616, he made himself useful in many ways to his countrymen. He died on the 16th May, 1620, and was buried on one of the hills overlooking Yedo harbour, where his grave may still be seen.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-138" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-138" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 376, note.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-139" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-139" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The works of the Rev. William Perkins (1558-1602), Fellow of Christ’s College, Cambridge, whose name is now almost forgotten, enjoyed at this time considerable popularity both in England and abroad, and he was regarded as one of the ablest exponents of the Calvinism then so much in vogue. His collected works were four times reprinted during the ten years succeeding his death. The “Book of Martyrs” is of course John Foxe’s “Actes and Monuments,” of which a sixth edition had just appeared.<br class="fn-para">Had Femell survived, and succeeded in establishing a permanent factory at Surat, as the Company hoped, these books would doubtless have formed the nucleus of a library there, such as was actually established at a later date. That the Company’s agents were not indifferent to literature, and especially to theological literature, is shewn by the following extract from a letter addressed to the Court by the President (Sir George Oxinden) and Council of Surat, under date 25th September, 1666:—<br class="fn-para">“Your Library here is carefully looked after and preserved, and wee could wish it “were better furnished with Boòkes. It consists for the maine of English Treatises “and is almost totally disfurnisht of the workes of the Ancient Writers; wee finde “not of all the ffathers workes, any more then the Epistles of Clemens Romanus “published by M<sup>r</sup> Patrick Young. here are the Epistles of Ignatius. The workes “of Epiphanius &amp; S<sup>t</sup> Augustine, with some few imperfect peeces of other ffathers,</span></li></ol></section>
